--- Page 2 ---
<meta>This is the title page for a 16th-century alchemical work published in Lyon. It establishes the authorial attribution to Pope John XXII and identifies the printer Macé Bonhomme.</meta>

# THE ELIXIR OF THE PHILOSOPHERS,

->OTHERWISE,<-

->*The <term>Art of Transmutation</term>, very useful, attributed to Pope JOHN XXII of that name: not yet seen, nor printed before now.*<-
<note>Transmutation refers to the alchemical process of changing base metals, like lead or copper, into noble metals like gold or silver. While this work is attributed to Pope John XXII (reigned 1316–1334), historians generally consider such attributions to be "pseudo-epigraphical," meaning the name of a famous figure was used to give the book more authority. In reality, John XXII issued a famous decree against alchemical fraudsters.</note>

<image-desc>An ornate printer's mark within a decorative cartouche. An oval medallion depicts a winged hero (likely Perseus) wielding a sword over a fallen beast (possibly a three-headed monster like Cerberus). The Greek motto "Glory from labor" <note>original: "ΕΚ ΠΟΝΟΥ ΚΛΕΟΣ"</note> is inscribed in the upper arch of the medallion. Below the central figure are several small human figures in various poses.</image-desc>

->AT LYON,<-

->By Macé Bonhomme,<-

->1557<-

->WITH THE <term>KING’S PRIVILEGE</term>.<-
<note>A King’s Privilege was an early form of copyright, granting the printer exclusive rights to publish the work for a set number of years to prevent unauthorized copies.</note>

<summary>This title page introduces an alchemical treatise on the "Elixir of the Philosophers" and the art of transmutation, attributed to Pope John XXII and published in Lyon in 1557. It features a prominent printer's mark for Macé Bonhomme with a Greek motto emphasizing the rewards of hard work.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, transmutation, Elixir of the Philosophers, Pope John XXII, Macé Bonhomme, Lyon, Renaissance printing, 16th century, Perseus, Greek motto</keywords>

<meta>This is the title page of a 1557 alchemical treatise, marking the beginning of the manuscript. It introduces the work as a previously unpublished text on transmutation attributed to a 14th-century Pope.</meta>

# THE ELIXIR
## OF THE PHILO-
## SOPHERS,

->OTHERWISE,<-

*The art of transmutation* <note>The alchemical process of changing base metals, like lead, into noble metals like gold or silver</note>, *most useful, attributed to Pope JOHN XXII* <note>John XXII (r. 1316–1334) was famously interested in finances and issued a bull against alchemical fraud, yet ironically many alchemical texts were attributed to him in the centuries following his death to lend them authority</note> *of that name: not yet seen, nor printed before now.*

<summary>This is the title page of a 1557 alchemical work attributed to Pope John XXII, printed in Lyon by Macé Bonhomme. It introduces the "art of transmutation" as a previously unpublished and highly useful secret of the "philosophers" (alchemists).</summary>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page of the 1557 edition of "The Elixir of the Philosophers." It provides the legal authorization for the book's publication and sale, establishing the printer's exclusive rights.</meta>

## Extract from the Privilege.

**BY** express privilege of the King our Lord <note>King Henry II of France (reigned 1547–1559).</note>, dated the eighth 
day of August in the year one thousand five hundred fifty-six, which
was published & registered in the court of the Seneschalcy <note>A royal court of justice and administrative district in Renaissance France.</note> 
of Lyon, permission has been granted to Macé Bonhomme, printer 
of Lyon, to print, and have printed in such characters <note>The specific fonts or typefaces used in the layout.</note>
as he shall see fit, to offer for sale, and to distribute the 
present book, titled, *the Elixir of the Philosophers, otherwise, the*
*transmutatory art <note>The alchemical process of changing base metals into gold or silver.</note> of Pope John XXII. of that name: not yet seen,*
*nor printed.* Whereby prohibitions are made to all booksellers 
& printers, and others, against printing, or having printed the 
said book in any form or manner whatsoever, or from those which 
may have been counterfeited, or printed elsewhere, nor to bring, 
nor expose for sale in the lands and territories of this Kingdom, 
during the time & term of ten years, starting from the day that 
the printing of the present book shall be completed, with heavy 
penalties against those who shall contravene directly, 
or indirectly the said privilege. By which it is permitted 
to insert, for all prohibitions and significations, the sum-
mary of the said privilege at the beginning or at the end of the pre-
sent book: just as is more fully contained in the pri-
vilege aforementioned.

->The first printing of the present book was<-
->completed on November 15, 1557.<-

<summary>This page contains the "Privilège du Roy," a legal copyright notice granting the printer Macé Bonhomme exclusive rights to publish and sell this alchemical work for ten years. It also records the official completion date of the first edition as November 15, 1557.</summary>
<keywords>privilege, copyright, Macé Bonhomme, Lyon, printing history, King Henry II, Pope John XXII, 1557</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of materials derived from the human body—specifically blood, hair, and urine—explaining why the "human microcosm" is a source for alchemical work. It transitions into the necessity of the purification process.</meta>

# The Elixir

- - -

[thin]gs <note>original: "fes"; the conclusion of "choses" (things) from the previous page.</note>, which come from man, because they have proven that man in his nature is one of the most accomplished and tempered <note>original: "attemperées"; referring to the ideal balance of the four elemental qualities (hot, cold, moist, dry).</note> creatures in the world. Therefore he is called man; whoever knows him well in his nature could find all the things of the world within him.

Now let us leave all other things and speak of the things that we must maintain in this chapter; and because they are worth nothing—or so one might think in another sense—if they were taken commonly without being cleaned and prepared, it is necessary for us to speak of the preparations, and how one prepares them, and makes them honest and pure <note>original: "mondes"; meaning alchemically cleansed or ritualistically purified.</note>, and the things it is necessary to add, and all their nature and preparation, in the manner of the division of this

<meta>catchword: science</meta>

<summary>This page explains that man is a "microcosm" containing all elements of the world, justifying the use of human-derived substances in alchemy. It emphasizes that these raw materials are worthless unless they are properly purified and prepared.</summary>

<keywords>microcosm, purification, human nature, alchemical preparation, tempered balance, ingredients</keywords>

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues from page 10, which concluded with the phrase "the division of this." This page begins with the word "science," completing the thought that the treatise will now describe the specific scientific operations required to prepare alchemical substances from the human microcosm.</meta>

### of the Philosophers.

science. And this science has eight necessary parts. First, the thing that one calls the **Elixir** (and see them all here): purging, sublimating, calcining, distilling, dissolving, congealing, waxing <note>original: "incerer"; from the Latin *inceratio*, a process of adding liquid to a substance to bring it to a soft, wax-like consistency.</note> or impasting, and casting <note>This refers to the "projection" or the final step of adding the Elixir to a base metal to transform it.</note> one's Elixir upon metals when they are prepared.

<margin>Purging</margin>
We call it **purging** when we remove from any substance with which we wish to work that nature which is contrary to our work <note>original: "ouuraige"; meaning the alchemical "Great Work" or the specific operation at hand.</note>.

<margin>Sublimating</margin>
We call it **sublimating** when a thing is mixed with others and finely ground <note>original: "triblée menuemẽt"; likely from the Latin *tribulare*, meaning to crush or grind.</note>, then placed in a suitable vessel over the fire, and it rises upward and falls onto the cover <note>original: "conuertoir"; the lid or head of the vessel, such as an alembic, where vapors condense.</note> of the vessel in the form of a powder; and every sublimation must be white, if it is rightly done; and nothing can properly sublimate, except

<meta>Catchword: that</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the eight essential operations of the alchemical "science" required to create the Elixir, providing specific definitions for "purging" (purification) and "sublimation" (the vaporization and recrystallization of a substance).</summary>

<keywords>alchemical operations, Elixir, purging, sublimation, inceration, distillation, calcination, laboratory practice</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of sublimation from page 11, focusing on the volatile "seven spirits" and the vessel required to capture them, before transitioning to a definition of calcination.</meta>

### The Elixir

than the seven spirits. We call
them spirits because they do not
remain upon the fire, but
rather vanish into the fire; and for this
reason the sages devised a vessel proper for this
wherein they might retain these spirits,
which thus rise in smoke, and it is
called an alterer or a sublimator.

<margin>Calcining</margin>
We call it **calcining** when
a thing has become a powder so
fine that one can barely
feel the particles; and everything
that has an earthly nature within it can be cal-
cined: for by fire and other things
that are dry and burning—such as
common salt, sulfur, and orpiment <note>A yellow mineral (arsenic trisulfide) used by alchemists because of its golden color and volatile properties.</note>—and
everything hot in its nature,
one can dry it out and extract
all moisture; thus nothing will remain
there except the earth, which we
<margin>Lime.</margin>
call lime <note>original: "chaulx"; in alchemy, the "calx" or lime is the dry, crumbly powder left after a metal or mineral has been thoroughly roasted or oxidized.</note>.

<summary>This page explains the nature of volatile alchemical "spirits" that require specialized vessels to be contained, and defines "calcining" as the process of reducing a substance to its earthly base or "lime" through intense heat and drying agents.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the definitions of alchemical processes from page 12. Having previously defined sublimation and calcination, the author now addresses distillation, resolution (dissolving), and begins the definition of congelation.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>
### of the Philosophers.

<margin>Distilling</margin>
**We** call it **distilling**, when a thing is placed in a vessel, and the moisture rises upward as a vapor all on its own, in the manner of rosewater, and then descends through the pipe <note>original: "tuel"; the tube or neck of a still through which vapor travels.</note> of a vessel, and this is the proper way to distill: but one may also distill by felt <note>A method of capillary filtration where a strip of fabric or felt draws liquid from one vessel to another, acting as a filter.</note>, as we shall speak of and teach hereafter.

<margin>Dissolving</margin>
We call it **dissolving** <note>original: "resoudre"; in alchemy, "resolution" is the process of turning a solid substance into a liquid state.</note>, when a dry and hard thing, whatever it may be, is converted into water. In this regard, one can find no substance that has been burnt to dryness and calcined by fire that does not easily convert into water; and it happens without the sun, and even by dung <note>original: "fiens"; horse dung was frequently used by alchemists as a source of gentle, steady, and long-lasting heat for digestion and dissolution.</note>, and all manner of salt also.

<margin>Congealing</margin>
We call it **congealing**, when a thing is converted into water, <note>The sentence ends abruptly here, continuing on the following page.</note>

<meta>catchword: and we</meta>

…and we wish to make it hard like powder or salt, and it is placed in glass vials set in white pots of ashes <note>An ash bath used to distribute heat evenly around the glass vessel.</note> over a small fire, until it is converted into hardness.

<margin>Incerating</margin>
We call it **incerating** <term>Inceration: the process of adding liquid to a dry substance to bring it to a soft, wax-like (from the Latin 'cera') state.</term> or **emplastering** <note>original: "emplastrer"; to make a substance pliable like a medicinal plaster.</note>, when one or several things are hard and dry by themselves, and lack the power to enter into metals, nor to mix or dissolve. We then grind <note>original: "triblons"; to crush or pulverize, likely using a muller on a stone slab.</note> them on a marble stone very evenly with certain waters or oils several times, until all the parts are as soft as **wax**. This must be done many times; then the substance is ready to be put to resolve <note>To be dissolved or melted back into a liquid state.</note>, for it would otherwise soon cool because of the parts that are diminished and saturated with water all around.

<margin>Purifying.</margin>

<margin>Purification of urine.</margin>

A general term for metallic ores, often iron pyrites.</note>, if they are well-purified. 

You shall purify marcasites and magnes thus: grind them very finely, and then boil them in **vinegar**, and in **water of tartar** <term>water of tartar</term> <note>original: "eau de grauelle." Literally "water of gravel," this refers to an acidic solution made from the tartar (potassium bitartrate) found in wine barrels.</note> for half a day in each, once more in vinegar for a little while, and then strain the vinegar and wash them with common water, and dry the powder, and keep them: for none were ever [properly] sublimed nor put into work [otherwise].

# The Elixir

**air** <note>This completes the word from the previous page. In this context, "air" refers to the volatile or gaseous component that has been refined into a white solid.</note> clean and white as snow, and then you shall keep it; and then put together all the **water** and the **fire** <note>In alchemical practice, "water" usually refers to the clear distilled spirit, while "fire" refers to a thick, often oily or colored substance that remains after the "water" is drawn off.</note> that are left to you and that you have gathered from the air, and distill it by a small fire enough times, until you have clear water from it; thus the fire will remain at the bottom of the **still** <note>original: "distillatoir."</note>. # The Elixir #

For **gold**, you shall do this: Take the **fire** <note>In this context, "fire" refers to the "fixed" or solid residue (the *feces*) remaining at the bottom of the still after the volatile components have been driven off.</note>, which will remain at the bottom of the **distilling vessel** after the distillation of the **oil of the three kinds**, and grind it well and strongly upon a **marble** <note>A flat marble slab used with a muller for grinding alchemical substances into a fine powder.</note>: and when it is ground: take two parts of that **fire**, and one part of the **oil of the three kinds**, and grind them well, and mix them together, and put them to **dissolve** <note>Original: "resoudre"—to liquefy or break down a solid into a liquid state through heat and moisture.</note> in a **glass vessel** and seal it well, and then set it to dissolve for **forty days** upon **horse dung**, and renew the dung every eight days <note>Rotting horse dung was used by alchemists as a source of gentle, consistent, and low-level heat for "digestion" or slow chemical reactions.</note>, and if it is dissolved within forty days it is good, if not, leave it in the dung until you find it all dissolved, and converted into **clear water**, and when it is all dissolved, put it in a **distilling vessel**, and distill it in **ashes** <note>An ash bath provided a more intense, dry heat than the moist heat of the dung bath.</note> as it is said in the **chapter of blood** <note>This likely refers to an earlier section regarding the "Blood of the Green Lion" or the distillation of a red-colored mineral spirit.</note>.

Calcining furnaces <term>calcine: to heat a substance to high temperatures, often to reduce it to a powder or ash through oxidation</term> must be very strong and thick <note>Original: "espois," meaning thick-walled to retain heat.</note>, and must be sealed <note>Original: "acier," which usually means "steel" but here suggests a hardened or airtight seal.</note> at the top, so that neither the smoke nor the flame can escape except through a hole <term>pertuis: a small opening or vent</term> which is on top; and on both sides of the furnace, higher than the middle, there must be two strong and level tiles <note>Original: "tuilles," likely ceramic plates or bricks used as an internal shelf.</note> upon which one shall place the substance that one wishes to calcine.

# The Elixir

<meta>catchword: A/One</meta>

<image-desc>A woodcut illustration within a rectangular frame depicting a tall, slender cylindrical vessel standing upright. The vessel is shaded with horizontal hatching to indicate its round form and appears to be set into a textured base, perhaps earth or clay.</image-desc>

The **dissolving vessel** <note>Original: "resolutoire"; a vessel used for the process of "resolution," which involves breaking a substance down into its primary components or dissolving it.</note> must be like a well-fitted box, with a tight lid, and made of strong earthenware <note>Original: "forte terre"; durable clay or ceramic.</note> and well-**glazed** <note>Original: "plombé"; referring to a lead-based glaze used to seal the porous ceramic.</note> on the inside if you wish: and here is the manner of it: and if you wish, you may certainly dissolve in a **glass flask** <note>Original: "fiole de voyrre."</note>: for glass retains [the substance] better.

<note>The author provides a choice between ceramic and glass for the process of dissolution. While earthenware was common and sturdy, the "resolutory" process often involved volatile spirits that could seep through porous clay, making the non-porous nature of glass ("voyrre") preferable for containment.</note>

<summary>This page describes the specifications for a dissolving vessel, recommending either well-glazed earthenware or a glass flask, noting that glass is superior for retaining the substances being processed.</summary>
<keywords>dissolving vessel, resolutoire, earthenware, glass flask, lead-glaze, alchemical apparatus, containment</keywords>

### This is the first chapter for performing the coagulation of Mercury:

Take one pound of native sulfur <term>native sulfur: (souphre vif) naturally occurring, pure sulfur, often called 'virgin sulfur'</term>, and grind it as finely as you can; then cook it for an entire day in eight pounds of lye <term>lye: (lessive) a strong alkaline solution used for cleaning or chemical reactions</term>. Make the [lye] with two parts pearl ash <term>pearl ash: (cendres gravelées) a potassium-rich salt produced by burning wood and vine branches</term> and the other part quicklime <term>quicklime: (chaux vive) calcium oxide, a caustic substance produced by heating limestone</term>. And as the lye decreases by boiling, add common boiling water into it; and when it has boiled for the whole day, strain it through a thick bag into a clean vessel of felt <note>original: "feutre"; felt was commonly used as a heavy-duty filter for caustic liquids</note> or earthenware. And when this is done, add four pints of good vinegar and four pints of cold water over it, and it will become white:

<margin>Powder of the sun <note>An alchemical name for gold powder, often used for gilding or as a transmutatory ingredient.</note>.</margin>
Gold is thus pulverized: Take a vessel wide at the bottom and narrow at the mouth, and inside melt some lead; and over the mouth place a plate, or a coin of fine gold, so that it may receive the smoke <note>The lead fumes act as a corrosive agent to make the gold brittle enough to grind.</note> of the lead. Take the aforementioned plate, turning it over and over above the said smoke, and shortly after you will find your gold ready to be pulverized and made into powder.

<term>Burnt copper</term> <note>original: "Aes vstum"; a chemical compound, usually copper(II) oxide, prepared by heating copper with sulfur or salt.</note> is pulverized in this way: pulverize it and wash this powder with plain water, or with salt water, as many times as necessary until it becomes dry; and then make a powder of it.

# Transmutation

That which remains at the bottom of the vessel is a good <term>Elixir</term> <note>A "philosopher's stone" in liquid form, intended to transform metals or prolong life.</note> for dyeing Saturn into the Sun <note>original: *tingens Saturnum in solem*; in alchemical terminology, Saturn represents Lead and the Sun represents Gold.</note>.

*If Saturn [Lead] is melted upon molten glass, it will be hard, and will withstand the fire in all circumstances.*

## The Multiplication of Mercury
Take a reed <note>original: *calamum*; a hollow tube or pipe used as a container.</note> and fill it with mercury, and make a small paste of sulfur; take this and place it all around the reed. Afterward, place it in a tall jar with <term>vitriol</term> <note>A sulfate mineral, typically iron or copper sulfate, highly valued for its reactive properties.</note> so that it sits in the middle and is entirely covered. Then, let it be placed in the furnace and heated well; but take care that it does not <term>sublimate</term> <note>To turn from a solid or liquid into vapor.</note> and then cool. You will find the Mercury has been turned red <note>original: *rubificatum*; the "reddening" of a substance was often considered a sign of its increasing power or maturity in the alchemical process.</note>.

Mix the ashes in equal parts and form a paste with pure water of <term>tartar</term> <note>A salt found in the sediment of wine casks, often used as a flux or cleaning agent in metallurgy.</note>. Then, place the <term>alembic</term> <note>original: *alembicum*; the cap or "still-head" used to collect and direct vapors during distillation.</note> on top and distill it. Afterward, return the water that has distilled to the vessel three or four times. That copper should then be melted with whatever residues you find in the cucurbit, and it will be as white as the moon <note>original: *album vt luna*; in the symbolic language of alchemy, the Moon represents Silver.</note>. If indeed it is not white, melt it twice, and after the melting, mix with it a fourth or fifth part of true moon [silver], and it will be of the best quality.

### An Excellent Water
Take <term>quicklime</term> <note>original: *calcem viuam*; calcium oxide, a caustic substance produced by heating limestone.</note> and alkali salt, and place them with oil and stir well. Place this in an alembic and distill it. With this water, soak white <term>arsenic</term> <note>original: *arcenicum album*; likely arsenic trioxide, used to "whiten" copper to make it look like silver.</note>, or white sulfur, or just <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: *argentumviuum*; Mercury.</note>, until you can place it upon a plate of copper and it melts. Place some of this upon molten copper, and it will become the moon [silver].⁴

### Incombustible Oil
Take <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>original: *sal armoniacum*; ammonium chloride, used in alchemy as a flux and to prepare various "spirits."</note> and <term>quicklime</term> <note>original: *calcem viuam*; calcium oxide, a caustic substance produced by heating limestone.</note>, and place them over oil. Leave it for 3 days, and then distill it. In the distillation, add a fourth part of glass and lime, and distill again. Soak whatever you wish with this until it flows, because the <note>original: *philosophi*; the alchemists often referred to themselves or the ancient masters of the art as "philosophers."</note> philosophers say that if you dissolve powder many times with this oil, it will immediately rise again and have <term>entry</term> <note>original: *ingressum*; the capacity of an alchemical substance to penetrate and merge into the body of a metal to change its nature.</note>. Others have said that if oil has been distilled with lime, it will not be burned; however, if you distill it with lime ten times, it will become a poison and destroy everything.

### The Calcination of Tin
Or <term>sublimation</term> <note>original: *sublimatio* or *sublimatura*; the process of heating a substance so it turns into vapor and then solidifies again, often used to purify materials.</note>. Sprinkle over the <term>tin</term> <note>original: *stanni*; often associated with the planet Jupiter in alchemical lore.</note> distilled strong vinegar until the filings are dissolved. This creates a very fine powder that is too delicate to be touched. Meanwhile, afterward, dissolve it with whatever <term>spirit</term> <note>original: *spiritu*; a volatile substance like an acid or alcohol, believed to be the "life force" or active essence of a material.</note> you wish...

### *To make quicksilver.* <note>original: *Ad faciendum argentum viuum*; Quicksilver is the traditional name for mercury.</note>

Take <term>lead</term> <note>original: *plumbum*; in the alchemical tradition, lead is the "base" metal associated with the planet Saturn.</note> and melt it in a pan. Afterward, separate its parts with whitened, powdered salt, stirring it continually with a spatula. When it has been indeed <term>calcined</term> <note>original: *calcinatum*; reduced to a powder through heat and oxidation.</note>, you shall separate the salt from what remains using hot water. Then, place the material with <term>marble lime</term> <note>original: *calce marmoris*; calcium oxide produced from crushed and burned marble</note> into a well-sealed glass vial. Place this vial in warm <term>horse manure</term> <note>original: *fimo equino*; a common alchemical method for maintaining a low, steady "digestive" heat over long periods.</note> for a month, replacing the manure every seven days. In this way, it is turned into quicksilver and into the substance—

### **The Resolution of Lead** <note>original: *Resolutio plumbi*; the process of dissolving or breaking a metal down into its primary liquid or salt form.</note>

Grind lead upon a <term>whetstone</term> <note>original: *cotem*; a stone used for grinding or sharpening, here used to create fine metallic particles.</note>, and rub it with a fourth part of its weight of <term>sal ammoniac</term> <note>original: *salis armoniaci*; ammonium chloride, a salt essential in alchemy for its ability to help dissolve metals and act as a flux.</note>. Moisten it while grinding with that water, or with simple water, and bury it in <term>manure</term> <note>original: *fimo*; typically horse manure, which alchemists used as a natural, low-heat "incubator" for long periods.</note>. By replacing the manure every five days, the material will liquefy. 

In this same way, <term>gold</term> <note>original: *aurum*</note>, <term>tin</term> <note>original: *stannum*</note>, and fixed <term>quicksilver</term> <note>original: *argentum vivum ligatum*; mercury that has been "bound" or solidified through chemical treatment</note> shall also be resolved, whether the substance be yellow, white, or red. Note that gold [should be treated] with <term>tragacanth water</term> <note>original: *aqua dragantis*; a mucilage made from the gum of the *Astragalus* plant, used as a binding agent.</note>. Let there hang over the whetstone an excessive whitening, or [else use] <term>orpiment</term> <note>original: *auripigmentum*; a deep yellow arsenic sulfide mineral, often used to attempt the coloring of metals to look like gold.</note>, quicksilver, and salt in three equal parts. After these have been mixed with vinegar and made into a paste, allow them to dry in the sun for nine days. Do not [proceed] without three parts of <term>sea foam</term> <note>original: *spumæ marinæ*; historically this referred to cuttlefish bone or certain porous mineral deposits found at the shore.</note>, as well as <term>iris alum</term> <note>original: *aluum adiridem*; likely *alumen irideum*, a specific mineral salt or "rainbow" alum mentioned in medieval chemical texts</note> joined to the mixture—

### **Concerning the water of lead, or of quicksilver**

Take lead filings and let them be immersed with half as much **Mercury** <note>original: *Mercurij*; commonly known as quicksilver.</note>, and a weight of common salt of the earth equal to both. <summary>This page details the extraction of "saffron of iron" by repeatedly quenching red-hot steel filings in vinegar, followed by a fifteen-day digestion and evaporation process. The resulting powder was a prized pigment and medicinal preparation in the early modern period.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for refining alchemical substances. <header>of transmutation</header>

### To prepare the said saffron of iron.

Take one ounce of the said **saffron of iron** <note>original: *crocum ferri*; a red oxide of iron used in both alchemy and as a pigment</note>, and 2 ounces of **native sulfur** <note>original: *souphre vif*; naturally occurring sulfur, often called brimstone</note>, and put it in a <term>luted crucible</term> <note>original: *croisel luté*; a ceramic vessel sealed with a paste or "lute" to make it airtight</note> as described above, and let there be a small hole in the lid: then place it in a <term>circular fire</term> <note>original: *in rota*; a method of heating where the vessel is surrounded by a ring of coals</note> for the space of 12 hours, then place it for 4 hours in a <term>reverberatory furnace</term> <note>a furnace designed to reflect heat down onto the substance</note> at a high heat: and let the lid be removed, and you will find your **saffron of iron**, bright red in color and <term>impalpable</term> <note>a powder so fine that its grains cannot be felt between the fingers</note>.

### Preparation of tutty.

Take the said **tutty** <note>original: *tutie*; a zinc oxide residue collected from the flues of furnaces, used in medicinal salves and metallurgy</note> and place it in a crucible to heat to redness five times, and <term>quench</term> <note>original: *destaignes*; to rapidly cool hot metal in liquid</note> it five times in vinegar.

Note that when the said **burnt copper** <note>original: *æs vstum*; copper that has been oxidized through heating</note> and the said **tutty** are quenched as described above, the **vinegar** is no longer useful <note>original: *ne vaut riē*; literally "is worth nothing," meaning the vinegar's chemical properties have been spent</note>.

<summary>This page provides specific chemical instructions for refining iron oxide (saffron of iron) through prolonged heating with sulfur, and for purifying tutty (zinc oxide) using repeated heating and quenching in vinegar. These processes were essential for creating "impalpable" powders used in alchemical transmutations and medicinal preparations.</summary>

<header>Art</header>

### Preparation of burnt copper.

Proceed exactly as you did for the tutty, or you may otherwise prepare it as follows: Take men’s urine and a handful of salt, and skim the said urine until it no longer foams. Then melt your <term>burnt copper</term> <note>original: *æs vstum*; copper that has been oxidized through repeated heating and cooling, often used as a coloring agent or in alloys</note> and cast it into the liquid four or five times, or as many times as needed for it to achieve a sufficiently good color. In this manner, the said <term>burnt copper</term> is greatly softened and is suitable to be mixed with <term>silver</term> <note>original: *lune*; the alchemical term for silver, associated with the moon</note>.

### Preparation of sal ammoniac.

Take the said salt and reduce it to powder. Then place it in a quart of boiling water to dissolve, and <term>filter it through felt</term> <note>original: *distilles par feutre*; in this period, "distilling" often meant any form of liquid purification, including filtering solids out through a cloth or felt cone</note>. Then evaporate it and congeal it over a very low fire until it is crystallized.

<meta>catchword: Prepa</meta>

<summary>This page describes methods for refining burnt copper using urine and salt to improve its color and malleability for alloying with silver, followed by a process for purifying sal ammoniac through dissolution and filtration.</summary>
<keywords>burnt copper, aes vstum, sal ammoniac, silver, purification, alchemy, metallurgy</keywords>

## Preparation of vitriol.
Put it to dissolve in urine, then evaporate it until completely dry over a slow fire. Then place it over a slow fire between two <term>crucibles</term> <note>original: *croisels*; heat-resistant vessels used for heating substances at high temperatures</note>, and it will turn red. Let it be placed in a large crucible over a low fire, and it will be <term>calcined</term> <note>reduced to a powder or oxide through the action of heat</note>.

## Preparation of rock alum.
Let it be heated to redness in a crucible five times and <term>quenched</term> <note>original: *destaignes*; the process of rapidly cooling a hot material in a liquid to alter its properties</note> in vinegar and linseed oil.

## Preparation of tutty of Alexandria.
Let <term>arsenic</term> <note>The heading specifies tutty (zinc oxide), but the instruction calls for arsenic. In early modern alchemy, these mineral substances were often processed using similar methods of heating and quenching</note> be heated to redness and quenched in vinegar five times.

Put it to boil in vinegar until no more foam appears on top of the said vinegar; and if any is present, remove it with the back of a spoon. Then, once this is done, remove the said vine-

### *Purification of Mercury.*

Wash the <term>Mercury</term> <note>Quicksilver; in alchemy, it was considered one of the three prime principles of matter</note> in strong vinegar three or four times, until no darkness or impurity appears; then pass it through a double linen cloth, and then doubled again, and thus it will be purified. Some, when they have passed it through the cloth, set it to soak in urine for two hours, and afterwards set it to soak in vinegar for two more hours; then drain the vinegar, and it will be purified.

### *To make Moon of Mercury.*

Take one <term>mark</term> <note>A traditional unit of weight, here defined as half a pound (approx. 244 grams)</note> of Mercury, which is half a pound, and put it in a <term>crucible</term> <note>original: *croissel*; a vessel made of ceramic or metal for melting substances</note> over the fire, and leave it until it begins to boil, and then put with it one ounce... <note>The text cuts off here; "Moon" in this context refers to the alchemical goal of creating silver</note>
